Banking Industry
In the eurozone, the slight upturn in lending to the private sector continued into the second quarter of 2017. Corporate lending
volumes were up 0.4 % year-on-year, while lending with households increased by 2.4 %. Deposits remained buoyant, however,
recording growth of 4.3 %. In Germany, corporate lending increased slightly by 3.1 % year-on-year, while the growth rate in the
mortgage and consumer lending remained largely unchanged at 4 % and at 0.8 %, respectively. Private-sector deposits at
German banks were up 5 % year-on-year despite the decline in interest rates to nearly zero or, for corporates, even into nega-
tive territory.

Lending in the U.S. in the second quarter of 2017 failed to recover from its slump in the first quarter of 2017. While lending
volumes increased slightly, the market is still far short of the growth rates recorded in 2016. On an annualized basis, corporate
lending grew by just 2.9 % – in June 2016 this figure had been 8.5 %. Lending to retail clients stagnated, following growth of
6.1 % in the previous year. In both cases, the poor performance was relatively uniform across all lending categories. The growth
in private-sector deposits also slowed, declining to just 3.1 % on an annualized basis. The figure had been double this amount
in the summer of 2016.

In Japan, the growth rates for lending and deposits converged to a certain extent in the second quarter of 2017, with the former
rising to 3.3 % year-on-year and the latter contracting to 4.4 %. Lending growth thus reached its highest level since 2009, while
deposit growth remained historically robust.

Deutsche Bank                                                           Operating and Financial Review
Interim Report as of June 30, 2017                                      Deutsche Bank Performance




China's credit boom continued unabated in the second quarter of 2017, with the pace of growth accelerating in some instances.
As had already been the case in the previous quarter, lending volumes to households were up 25 % year-on-year on the back
of a significant increase in medium- and long-term lending. The growth rate for corporate lending increased, with the outstand-
ing volume rising by 11 % year-on-year. Overall, private-sector lending is at its most dynamic since 2011 and the lending vol-
ume has also roughly doubled since then. In respect of funding, deposits lost momentum slightly over the past few months but
the figure remains in the double-digit percentage range above the prior-year level. However, the gap between deposits and
lending is at its lowest absolute level in a decade; since the financial crisis, the ratio of lending to deposits has climbed from a
low of 61 % to its current level of over 90 % due to increased levels of debt.



Deutsche Bank Performance
In the second quarter of 2017 Deutsche Bank’s performance was mainly influenced by a decline in revenues, driven by a num-
ber of factors, notably the market environment and the strategic and business decisions we have made. The decline in reve-
nues was more than offset by lower credit loss provisions and lower noninterest expenses driven by lower litigation expenses
and impairments, as compared to the second quarter of 2016. Lower noninterest expenses also reflect cost management ef-
forts, disposals and the impact from NCOU which ceased to exist from January 1, 2017. With the implementation of the new
divisional structure and the capital increase of approximately € 8 billion in April, we made a significant step forward on the path
to create a simpler, stronger and growing bank.

The new divisional structure as per our strategy announcement in March 2017 is reflected in our reporting for the second quar-
ter of 2017. Prior period data have been restated to reflect the new segment structure. Accordingly the business model is simpli-
fied and consists of three divisions – Corporate and Investment Bank (CIB), Private & Commercial Bank (PCB) and Deutsche
Asset Management (Deutsche AM). As mentioned above, NCOU ceased to exist from 2017 onwards and only its 2016 financial
information continues to be included in this report.

2017 to 2016 Three Months Comparison

In the second quarter of 2017 revenues decreased primarily due to lower client activity resulting from low volatility in the market
and declining margins. The bank’s result was also negatively impacted by a tightening of own credit spreads and the disposal of
certain of Deutsche Bank’s subsidiaries. The decrease in revenues was more than offset by a decrease in noninterest expens-
es and lower provisions for credit losses.

Group net revenues in the second quarter of 2017 decreased by € 770 million, or 10 %, to € 6.6 billion. In CIB revenues de-
clined primarily due to low client activity resulting from a low volatility environment impacting Sales and Trading revenues. The
tightening of margins negatively impacted Global Transaction Banking revenues. Net revenues in PCB decreased primarily
related to the nonrecurrence of positive impacts from disposals recorded in the second quarter of 2016 and the continued nega-
tive impact of low interest rates. Deutsche AM revenues decreased mainly due to the nonrecurrence of gains resulting from a
write-up relating to HETA Asset Resolution AG (HETA) and the absence of mark to market movements on policyholder posi-
tions in Abbey Life, recorded in the second quarter of 2016. Excluding these effects, revenues in Deutsche AM were higher
compared to the second quarter of 2016, mainly driven by Alternatives performance fees and management fees on improved
market conditions.

Provision for credit losses was € 79 million in the second quarter of 2017, a decrease of € 181 million, or 70 %, compared to the
same period in 2016. This reduction was primarily driven by CIB, due to broad based improved performance, though the ship-
ping sector continued to prove challenging. Additional reductions in PCB were driven by a release in Postbank.

Noninterest expenses were € 5.7 billion in the second quarter of 2017, a decrease of € 1.0 billion, or 15 % as compared to the
second quarter of 2016. The reduction in noninterest expenses mainly reflect lower restructuring and impairment costs, the
effects from disposals and the closure of NCOU as well as lower litigation and cost management efforts. Compensation and
benefits amounted to € 2.9 billion, down by € 38 million or 1 %. The impact of headcount reductions was largely offset by higher
accruals for variable compensation. General and administrative expenses were € 2.7 billion, down by € 497 million or 15 %
compared to the second quarter of 2016, mainly due to lower litigation charges, the effects from disposals, the closure of NCOU
and lower professional service fees and IT costs. Restructuring costs were € 64 million, a decrease of € 115 million as com-
pared to second quarter of 2016. The decrease was due to phasing of restructuring measures.




4
Deutsche Bank                                     Operating and Financial Review
Interim Report as of June 30, 2017                Consolidated Results of Operations




Income before income taxes was € 822 million in the second quarter of 2017, an increase of € 415 million or 102 % as com-
pared to the second quarter of 2016. The improvement is mainly attributable to lower noninterest expenses and lower provi-
sions for credit losses.

Net income in the second quarter of 2017 was € 466 million, compared to € 20 million in the second quarter of 2016. Income tax
expense in the current quarter was € 357 million versus € 388 million in the second quarter of 2016. The effective tax rate of 43 %
was mainly impacted by non-deductible expenses related to our footprint rationalization. The prior year’s quarter effective tax
rate was 95 %.

2017 to 2016 Six Months Comparison

The first six months of 2017 were impacted by negative effects related to debt valuation adjustments (DVA, which reflects the
impact of the Group’s own credit risk in the fair value of derivative contracts) and the valuation of own debt as well as by a
challenging market environment in the second quarter of 2017. The decline in revenues was more than offset by a decrease in
noninterest expenses and lower provisions for credit losses.

Group net revenues in the first six months of 2017 decreased by € 1.5 billion, or 10 % to € 14.0 billion. The decrease in reve-
nues was primarily due to a negative swing of more than € 800 million year-on-year, mainly resulting from a narrowing in
Deutsche Bank’s credit spreads in the first six months, compared with a widening of those credit spreads in the first six months
of 2016, this impacted both, derivative DVA in CIB and the bank’s own debt in C&A. Revenues in CIB were also impacted by
low client activity in a low volatility environment and the tightening of margins. The decrease in net revenues in PCB was pri-
marily related to the nonrecurrence of positive impacts from disposals, a reduced revenue base after the sale of the PCS busi-
ness recorded in 2016 and the continued negative impact from low interest rates. This was partially offset by gains from workout
activities in the Sal. Oppenheim franchise. In Deutsche AM revenues decreased primarily due to the nonrecurrence of one-off
gains on the sale of Asset Management India and a write-up relating to HETA and absence of mark-to-market movements on
policyholder positions recorded in 2016. Excluding these effects, revenues in Deutsche AM were higher mainly driven by Alter-
natives performance fees and management fees on improved market conditions.

Provision for credit losses was € 212 million in the first six months of 2017, a decrease of € 352 million, or 62 % compared to
the same period 2016, resulting from reductions in CIB, due to broad based improved performance, though the shipping sector
continued to prove challenging. Further reductions resulted from PCB driven by a release in Postbank as well as from the non-
recurrence of items related to assets reported under NCOU in the prior year.

Noninterest expenses were € 12.0 billion in the first six months of 2017, a decrease of € 1.9 billion, or 13 % as compared to the
first six months of 2016. The reduction in noninterest expenses reflects mainly lower impairment, restructuring and litigation cost,
the effects from disposals and the closure of NCOU and cost management efforts. Compensation and benefits amounted to €
6.1 billion, down by € 85 million or 1 %. The impact of headcount reductions and lower severance cost were largely offset by
higher cost for variable compensation. General and administrative expenses were € 5.9 billion, down by € 1.0 billion or 15 %
compared to the first six months of 2016, mainly due to lower litigation charges while significant additional charges related to
litigation and regulatory actions are possible in future periods as conditions change, the effects from disposals and the closure
of NCOU and lower professional service fees. Restructuring costs were € 50 million, a decrease of € 339 million as compared
to the first six months of 2016. The first six months of 2017 included a release of restructuring provisions which reflected the
disciplined execution of reorganisation measures in the PCB business in Germany.

Income before income taxes was € 1.7 billion in the first six months of 2017, an increase of € 714 million or 72 % as compared
to the first six months of 2016. The improvement is mainly attributable to lower noninterest expenses and lower provisions for
credit losses.

Net income in the first six months of 2017 was € 1.0 billion, compared to € 256 million in the first six months of 2016. Income tax
expense in the first six months of 2017 was € 660 million versus € 731 million in the first six months of 2016. The effective tax
rate of 39 % was mainly impacted by non-deductible expenses related to our footprint rationalization. In the first six months of
2016 the effective tax rate was 74 %.

As announced on March 5, the Bank has reorganized its divisional structure and the below reflects the new segmental reporting
structure for the CIB division. CIB comprises the former Global Markets (GM), Corporate Finance (CF) and Global Transaction
Banking (GTB) businesses. In the new reporting structure, GM’s Financing businesses will be shown separately, and includes
certain activities previously reported in ‘Sales & Trading debt and other products’ and Loan products. Generally it includes more
stable revenues where the business objective is to hold assets rather than originate to sell or manage in the trading book. Fur-
thermore, legacy will be reported under ‘Other’. In line with market practice, Credit Valuation Adjustment (CVA) and Funding
Valuation Adjustment (FVA) impacts are not discussed separately. These are reported within Sales & Trading.

2017 to 2016 Three Months Comparison

CIB net revenues for the second quarter of 2017 were € 3.6 billion, a decrease of € 704 million or 16 % year-on-year. During the
second quarter of 2017 despite continued macroeconomic and geopolitical uncertainties, financial markets experienced very
low volatility, which negatively impacted client activity levels. Primary capital markets activity, while still higher than the previous
year, has slowed down from the levels seen in the first quarter of 2017.

Global Transaction Banking net revenues were € 1 billion, a decrease of € 130 million or 12 %, impacted by tightened margins
including a change in the funding allocation methodology. Cash Management revenues were slightly lower, as recent interest
rate increases in the U.S. offset the negative impact of client and product perimeter reductions in 2016. Trade revenues were
lower driven by tighter margins. Trust, Agency and Securities Services revenues were essentially flat, as lower transaction
volumes due to client and country exits were offset by interest rate increases in the U.S.

Origination and Advisory generated net revenues of € 563 million in the second quarter 2017, a decrease of € 43 million, or 7 %.
Debt Origination revenues were 24 % lower compared to the prior year quarter reflecting lower client activity in the U.S. Equity
Origination revenues were 7 % lower reflecting a slowdown in industry volumes following a very strong first quarter in 2017.
Revenues in Advisory were 91 % higher, with a number of transactions closed during the quarter.




8
Deutsche Bank                                     Operating and Financial Review
Interim Report as of June 30, 2017                Corporate Divisions




Financing net revenues were € 554 million, 5 % lower compared to the second quarter of 2016, with good performance across
asset backed lending and commercial real estate offset by lower revenues from our investment grade lending business.

Sales & Trading (FIC) net revenues were € 1.1 billion, a decrease of € 158 million or 12 %. Credit revenues were significantly
higher, supported by strong performance in distressed products in Asia. Rates revenues were lower driven by a difficult quarter
for market making in our U.S. Rates business. Foreign Exchange revenues were lower compared to a strong prior year quarter
which benefitted from high activity levels around the UK referendum on EU membership, whilst client activity in the current
quarter was impacted by very low levels of volatility. Revenues in Emerging Markets were lower across the covered regions.
Foreign exchange and rates Asia Pacific revenues were significantly lower mainly driven by reduced client activity.

Sales & Trading (Equity) generated net revenues of € 537 million, a decrease of € 205 million, or 28 %. Prime Finance reve-
nues were significantly lower reflecting lower margins and lower client balances. However, balances at the end of the current
quarter 2017 were higher than the levels in September 2016, but remained below the levels recorded in the second quarter of
2016. Equity Derivatives revenues were slightly lower as client flow was impacted by the low volatility environment and Cash
Equities revenues were lower due to sluggish client volumes.

Other revenues saw a loss of € 143 million, compared to a loss of € 3 million in 2016. These included a loss of € 104 million
(2016: a loss of € 11 million) relating to the impact of DVA on certain derivative liabilities. Revenues associated with assets
identified as portfolios not consistent with our new corporate led CIB strategy are now reported under ‘other’ as of the second
quarter 2017.

Provision for credit losses decreased by € 99 million or 64 %.The reduction was attributable to broad based improved perfor-
mance despite continued challenges in shipping.

Noninterest expenses were € 3 billion, a decrease of € 705 million or 19 % compared to the prior year quarter. This was mainly
driven by reduced litigation provisions and materially lower goodwill impairment compared to the second quarter of 2016. Non
compensation costs were also lower due to reduced professional service fees, while the second quarter of 2016 also included
Deutsche Bank’s share of an industry wide voluntary remediation scheme for derivatives sold to SME clients in the Netherlands
prior to the acquisition of the respective business.

Income before income taxes was € 543 million (2016: income € 460 million). The year-on-year reduction in revenues was more
than offset by reduced noninterest expenses and lower credit loss provisions.

2017 to 2016 Six Months Comparison

Revenues in the first half of 2017 were € 8 billion, down € 934 million or 10 % on the first half of 2016. The impact of a DVA loss
of € 323 million on certain derivative liabilities (2016: a gain of € 191 million), including the impact of a methodology change in
the first quarter of 2017 was a significant driver of this development. The first half of 2016 also included gains reported under
Sales & Trading related to the tender offer to repurchase senior unsecured debt securities.

Global Transaction Banking net revenues were € 2 billion, a decrease of € 190 million or 9 %, impacted by tightened margins
including a change in the funding allocation methodology. Cash Management revenues were essentially flat, as recent interest
rate increases in the U.S. and growth within its FX business offset the negative impact of client and product perimeter reduc-
tions in 2016. Trade revenues were lower driven by margin pressure. Trust, Agency and Securities Services revenues were
essentially flat, as lower transaction volumes due to client and country exits were offset by interest rate increases in the U.S.

Origination and Advisory revenues were € 1.2 billion, a € 104 million or 9 % increase compared to the prior year. Debt Origina-
tion revenues were essentially flat, with a strong first quarter of 2017 supported by high inflows into the Leveraged Loan market,
offset by reduced revenues in the second quarter of 2017 amid lower client activity and issuance volumes. Equity Origination
revenues were 43 % higher, benefitting from increased issuance volumes in 2017 in particular within the IPO market where
Deutsche Bank has been a key player. Advisory revenues were 12 % higher driven by a strong pipeline build and solid industry
fundamentals.

Financing net revenues were € 1.1 billion, 5 % lower than the first half of 2016 with good performance across asset backed
lending and commercial real estate offset by lower revenues from our investment grade lending business.




                                                                                                                                 9
Deutsche Bank                                       1 – Management Report
Interim Report as of June 30, 2017




Sales & Trading (FIC) net revenues were € 2.8 billion, an increase of € 58 million or 2 %. Credit revenues were significantly
higher supported by strong client activity in distressed products in the first half of 2017, while challenging market conditions in
2016 impacted flow businesses. Rates revenues were higher compared to the prior year as a result of a challenging market
environment, particularly in the first quarter of 2016. Foreign Exchange revenues were lower as a low volatility environment,
particularly in the second quarter of this year, impacted client flows. Emerging Markets revenues were lower across the covered
regions. Foreign exchange and rates Asia Pacific revenues were essentially flat with a strong first quarter of 2017 offset by
lower client activity in the second quarter of 2017.

Sales & Trading (Equity) generated net revenues of € 1.2 billion, a decrease of € 277 million, or 18 %. Revenues in Prime Fi-
nance were significantly lower reflecting lower margins and lower client balances. However, at the end of the current quarter
2017, these balances were higher than the levels in September 2016, but they remained below the levels recorded in the first
half of 2016. Equity Derivatives revenues were higher compared to a challenging prior year. Cash Equities revenues were
essentially flat with lower client volumes persisting into 2017.

Other revenues were a loss of € 384 million, compared to a gain of € 192 million in 2016, and included a loss of € 323 million
(2016: a gain of € 191 million) relating to the impact of DVA on certain derivative liabilities. € 136 million of this loss was due to a
change in the creditor hierarchy in the event of a bank insolvency which was introduced by the German Resolution Mechanism
Act (Abwicklungsmechanismusgesetz), effective January 1, 2017. This hierarchical change results in derivative counterparties
receiving greater protection as they would be satisfied prior to senior unsecured debt holders in the creditor waterfall structure.
This greater protection increases the value of the derivative assets for the counterparty, thereby increasing the value of deriva-
tive liabilities on our balance sheet, resulting in the loss. Revenues associated with assets identified as portfolios not consistent
with our new corporate led CIB strategy are now reported under ‘other’ as of the second quarter of 2017 as well.

Provision for credit losses decreased by € 192 million or 63 %. The reduction was attributable to broad based improved perfor-
mance despite continued challenges in shipping.

Noninterest expenses of € 6.6 billion, were € 819 million or 11 % lower than in the first half of 2016. Reduced litigation provi-
sions, materially lower Goodwill impairment and lower severance and restructuring all positively impacted the comparison ver-
sus the prior year. Compensation and non-compensation costs were also lower compared to the prior year.

Income before income taxes of € 1.2 billion was 7 % higher than in the previous year. The period-over-period reduction in reve-
nues was more than offset by reduced noninterest expenses and lower credit loss provisions.

Starting the second quarter 2017, Private & Commercial Bank (PCB) is established as a new segment combining the former
Private, Wealth & Commercial Clients and Postbank segments.

2017 to 2016 Three Months Comparison

PCB recorded income before income taxes of € 310 million in the second quarter of 2017. The year-on-year decline was mainly
due to specific factors in the second quarter 2016, which included gains from the sale of stakes in VISA Europe Limited in part
offset by restructuring charges. The second quarter 2017 had two specific revenue items, which largely offset one another.
Wealth Management’s revenues benefited from workout activities in the Sal. Oppenheim franchise and Postbank’s revenues
were negatively impacted by the termination of a legacy Trust Preferred Security. Provision for credit losses were on a very low
level in the current quarter, driven by selective portfolio sales and a provision release in Postbank. PCB’s business performance
excluding these factors was resilient compared to the second quarter last year.

Net revenues of € 2.6 billion decreased by € 207 million, or 7 %, compared to the previous year quarter mainly driven by the
aforementioned gains from the sale of stakes in VISA Europe Limited, which occurred in both the Private & Commercial Client
(PCC) businesses and in Postbank. In the PCC businesses, the gain from the sale of stakes in VISA Europe Limited amounted
to € 88 million and was the primary reason for a year-on-year decline in revenues of € 60 million, or 4 %. The current quarter
benefited to a lower extent from a specific sales transaction in Italy. Net interest income in the PCC businesses remained large-
ly stable. The low interest rate environment led to a continued decline in deposit product revenues, which was compensated by
other products reflecting positive internal funding effects. Commission and fee income remained stable compared to the prior
year quarter. Lower sales of insurance products including insurances for payment protection in the consumer finance business
were compensated by better investment product revenues reflecting our continued sales focus on these products. Revenues in
the Postbank businesses decreased by € 177 million, or 20 %, compared to the second quarter of 2016. The decline resulted
from the non-recurrence of the gains from the sale of stakes in VISA Europe Limited of € 104 million in the second quarter of
last year and a negative impact of € 118 million related to the termination of a legacy trust preferred security in the current quar-
ter. Excluding these effects, Postbank’s revenues slightly increased driven by higher commission and fee income mainly in
account products reflecting the introduction of a new pricing model. Net interest income decreased due to the impact of the low
interest rate environment on deposit revenues, which was partly mitigated by higher loan revenues due to increased loan vol-
umes. Revenues in the Wealth Management (WM) businesses increased by € 35 million, or 7 %, mainly driven by € 135 million
gains from workout activities in the Sal. Oppenheim franchise, which more than compensated for the impact of a lower revenue
base after the sale of the Private Client Services (PCS) unit in the third quarter of 2016. Excluding these factors, WM’s reve-
nues declined compared to the second quarter last year mainly driven by lower net interest revenues due to a lower deposit
base, a decline in lending volumes in the Americas as well as selective loan sales. Commission and fee income slightly de-
creased year-over-year.

Provision for credit losses of € 22 million decreased by € 79 million, or 78 %, compared to prior year quarter driven by a provi-
sion release in Postbank and selective portfolio sales in a continued benign economic environment.

Noninterest expenses of € 2.2 billion declined by € 70 million, or 3 %, compared to the second quarter of 2016, mainly due to a
€ 68 million lower impact from restructuring activities. Noninterest expenses in the current quarter were also positively impacted
by a reduced cost base after the disposal of the PCS unit and by savings from executed reorganisation measures. These cost-
reducing effects were partly offset by higher regulatory and investment spend as well as by higher accruals for performance-
related compensation.

Income before income taxes of € 310 million decreased by € 57 million compared to the second quarter of 2016. The decrease
was driven by the aforementioned gains from the sale of stakes in VISA Europe Limited in prior year period, in part compen-
sated by a lower impact from restructuring activities in the current quarter.

PCB’s Invested Assets of € 500 billion decreased by € 5 billion compared to March 31, 2017. Negative impacts of € 8 billion
from foreign exchange translation and market appreciation were partly compensated by € 3 billion net inflows, which mainly
occurred in deposit products.

2017 to 2016 Six Months Comparison

PCB’s result improved significantly compared to the first half of last year. This was driven by specific factors in both periods.
The first half 2016 benefited from the sale of stakes in VISA Europe Limited as well as from a dividend payment from one of
PCC’s shareholdings subsequent to a sales transaction by the investee in 2015. These positive impacts were partly offset by a
negative net valuation impact relating to the stake in Hua Xia Bank Co. Ltd. and restructuring charges. The first half 2017 bene-
fited from workout activities in WM’s Sal. Oppenheim franchise, from a net release of restructuring provisions in the PCC busi-
nesses and from a release of provision for credit losses in Postbank. These positive effects were partly offset by a negative
impact from the termination of a legacy trust preferred security in Postbank. PCB’s business performance excluding these
specific factors was solid in the first half of 2017.

Net revenues of € 5.3 billion decreased by € 102 million, or 2 %, compared to the prior year period impacted by above men-
tioned revenue items in both periods. In the Private & Commercial Client (PCC) businesses, revenues decreased by
€ 125 million, or 5 %, compared to the first half of 2016, which included a € 88 million positive impact from the sale of the stake
in VISA Europe Limited reflected in remaining income. The first half of 2017 benefited to a lower extent from a specific sales
transaction in Italy. Net interest income decreased compared to the first half of 2016, which included the aforementioned
€ 50 million dividend payment from one of PCC’s shareholdings. Beyond that, the ongoing low interest rate environment led to a
decline in PCC’s deposit product revenues. Commission and fee income slightly increased driven by better investment product
revenues reflecting our sales focus in these products. Revenues in the Postbank businesses decreased by € 266 million, or
15 %, compared to the first half of 2016 due to the termination of a legacy trust preferred security in the first half of 2017 with a
negative impact of € 118 million and the non-recurrence of the benefit from the sale of a stake in VISA Europe Limited of
€ 104 million in the first half of 2016. Net interest income declined due to the impact of the low interest rate environment on
deposit revenues which was partly mitigated by higher loan revenues as a result of increased loan volumes. Commission and
fee income increased mainly in current accounts reflecting the introduction of new pricing models. Beyond that, investment
product revenues also increased compared to the prior year period. Revenues in the Wealth Management (WM) businesses
increased by € 171 million, or 17 %, mainly driven by € 310 million gains from workout activities in the Sal. Oppenheim fran-
chise. These effects more than compensated the impact from the lower revenue base after the sale of the Private Client Ser-
vices (PCS) unit in the third quarter of 2016. Excluding these factors, WM’s revenues declined compared to the first half of last
year with decreased net interest revenues mainly due to a lower deposit base as well as selective loan sales. Commission and
fee income remained stable compared to the prior year period. Hua Xia revenues included a non-recurring negative valuation
impact of € 118 million in the first half of 2016 related to the stake in Hua Xia Bank Co. Ltd.

Provision for credit losses of € 100 million decreased by € 79 million, or 44 %, compared to the prior year period benefiting from
a provision release in Postbank and a continued benign economic environment. Both periods included positive impacts from
selective portfolio sales.

Noninterest expenses of € 4.5 billion declined by € 180 million, or 4 %, compared to the first half of 2016, mainly due to a
€ 172 million decline in restructuring charges. The first half of last year included net restructuring charges of € 120 million
whereas the first half of 2017 benefited from a net release of restructuring provisions of € 52 million which reflected the disci-
plined execution of the reorganization measures in the PCC business in Germany. Noninterest expenses in the first half of 2017
were also positively impacted by the reduced cost base after the disposal of the PCS unit and by savings of executed reorgani-
sation measures. These cost-reducing effects were partly offset by higher infrastructure and investment spend for digitalization,
regulatory projects and other initiatives as well as by higher accruals for performance-related compensation.

Income before income taxes of € 710 million increased by € 158 million compared to the first half of 2016. The increase was
driven by the aforementioned specific revenue factors regarding VISA Europe Limited, Hua Xia Bank Co. Ltd., the Sal. Oppen-

provision for credit losses.

PCB’s Invested Assets of € 500 billion increased by € 4 billion compared to December 31, 2016, driven by € 5 billion net inflows
(€ 2 billion in WM, € 3 billion in PCC businesses). The net inflows mainly occurred in deposit products partly driven by the suc-
cessful win-back of mandates after outflows in the third and fourth quarter of 2016 subsequent to the negative market percep-
tions associated with Deutsche Bank. Market and foreign exchange related effects largely offset each other.

and contains the asset management activities of Deutsche Bank. It focuses on providing investment solutions to individual
investors and institutions that serve them.

2017 to 2016 Three Months Comparison

Deutsche AM reported a strong performance in the second quarter of 2017, continuing the positive momentum from the start of
the year driven by improved market conditions compared to the prior year period. Net new assets also saw consecutive quarter-
ly inflows led by Passive and Active.

Net revenues were € 676 million, a decrease of € 29 million, or 4 %. Management fees increased by € 37 million, or 7 % driven
by Active reflecting improved market conditions. Performance and transaction fees increased significantly by € 61 million, due to
higher fund performance fees from Alternatives. Other revenues fell by € 56 million, or 79 % compared to prior year quarter
which benefitted from the write-up relating to HETA Asset Resolution AG exposure (HETA). Following the sale of Abbey Life in
the fourth quarter 2016 no mark-to-market movements on policyholder positions were recorded, compared to € 71 million, in the
second quarter 2016.

Noninterest expenses of € 442 million decreased by € 93 million or 17 %, due to the non-recurrence of costs relating to Abbey

Income before income taxes was € 234 million, an increase of € 64 million, or 38 %, driven by lower noninterest expenses,
partly offset by slightly lower revenues compared to the prior year benefiting from several one-off items including Abbey Life
mark-to-market movements and write up relating to HETA.

Invested assets were € 711 billion as of June 30, 2017, a decrease of € 12 billion versus March 31, 2017, driven by unfavoura-
ble foreign currency movements of € 20 billion, partly offset by net asset inflows of € 6 billion and favourable market develop-
ment of € 4 billion.

2017 to 2016 Six Months Comparison

During the first six months of 2017, Deutsche AM reported solid performance driven by improved market conditions compared
to the prior year. Compared to the net asset outflows reported in the prior year, the first six months of 2017 saw a positive turna-
round with € 11 billion net asset inflows led by the Germany, EMEA and APAC regions.

Net revenues in the first six months of 2017 were € 1.3 billion, a decrease of € 112 million, or 8 %. Management fees increased
by € 59 million, or 5 %, driven by Active reflecting favourable market movements. Performance and transaction fees increased
significantly by € 58 million, due to higher fund performance fees from Alternatives during the second quarter. Other revenues
decreased by € 114 million, or 74 % compared to the prior year which benefitted from the proceeds of the sale of Asset Man-
agement India and the write-up relating to HETA. Following the sale of Abbey Life in the fourth quarter 2016, no mark-to-market
movements on policyholder positions were recorded, compared to € 114 million reported in the first six months of 2016.

Noninterest expenses of € 868 million decreased by € 195 million, or 18 %, compared to the first six months of 2016, driven by
the non-recurrence of costs relating to Abbey Life, lower General and administrative expenses and significantly lower restructur-
ing and severance costs, partly offset by higher compensation and benefit costs.

Income before income taxes was € 414 million, an increase of € 83 million, or 25 % compared to the first six months of 2016,
driven by the aforementioned lower noninterest expenses, partly offset by lower revenues due to several one-off items in the
prior year including the sale of Asset Management India and the write-up relating to HETA .

Invested assets were € 711 billion as of June 30, 2017, an increase of € 5 billion versus December 31, 2016, driven by favoura-
ble market development of € 16 billion and net asset inflows of € 11 billion, partly offset by unfavourable foreign currency
movements of € 22 billion.

2017 to 2016 Three Months Comparison

Loss before income taxes in C&A was € 265 million in the second quarter of 2017, compared to a gain of € 42 million in the
prior year quarter. This decrease was mainly due to negative € 164 million in the second quarter 2017 resulting from the realiza-
tion of currency translation adjustments related to Deutsche Bank’s subsidiaries in Argentina and Uruguay, and the loss on sale
of the Argentina subsidiary. The second quarter 2017 also included negative € 3 million in valuation and timing differences
driven by gains on interest related items and cross currency basis largely offset by negative revenues resulting from the narrow-
ing of own credit spread, compared to positive € 71 million in prior year period. Additionally, revenues included negative € 30
million from municipal bonds taxable equivalent which is reported by the business and balanced out via C&A. Lastly, the prior
year quarter benefited from € 73 million insurance recoveries related to the Kirch settlement agreements.

2017 to 2016 Six Months Comparison

Loss before income taxes in C&A was € 672 million in the first half of 2017, compared to a gain of € 99 million in the prior year
first half. This decrease was mainly due to negative € 190 million in valuation and timing differences in 2017 resulting from a
significant narrowing of our own credit spread, compared to positive € 243 million in prior year period when the bank’s credit
spreads widened. The decrease also included negative € 167 million in the first half of 2017 due to realisation of currency trans-
lation adjustments related to Deutsche Bank’s subsidiaries in Argentina, Uruguay and New Zealand, and the loss on sale of the
Argentina subsidiary. Additionally, revenues included negative € 59 million from municipal bonds taxable equivalent which is
reported by the business and balanced out via C&A, and negative € 29 million as a result of a hedge related to the sale of Hua
Xia Bank stake.

Movements in Assets
As of June 30, 2017, total assets decreased by € 21.8 billion (or 1 %) compared to year-end 2016.

The overall decrease was primarily driven by a € 88.8 billion decrease in positive market values from derivative financial instru-
ments, mainly attributable to foreign exchange rate products.

Loans decreased by € 10.2 billion mainly driven by maturities during the normal course of business and targeted client repay-
ments.

These decreases were partly offset by an increase in cash and central bank balances together with interbank balances by
€ 43.7 billion, predominantly driven by reduction in loans as discussed above, increase in deposits and proceeds from our
capital raise.

Brokerage and securities related receivables increased by € 20.4 billion, mainly driven by higher receivables from unsettled
regular way trades following the seasonality pattern we typically observe of lower year-end levels versus higher volumes over
the course of the year, partially offset by lower collateral requirements corresponding to the decrease in negative market values
from derivative financial instruments.

Trading assets increased by € 17.1 billion primarily driven by debt securities mainly due to increased bond positions in EU & US
rates business and increased client activity, in line with seasonal patterns.

Central bank funds sold, securities purchased under resale agreements and securities borrowed, under both accrual and fair
value accounting, increased by € 4.6 billion, driven by increased firm financing activities and increased client flow.

The overall movement of the balance sheet included a decrease of € 45.3 billion due to foreign exchange rate movements
mainly driven by strengthening of the Euro versus the U.S. Dollar. The effects from foreign exchange rate movements are also
reflected in the development of the balance sheet line items discussed in this section.




16
Deutsche Bank                                      Operating and Financial Review
Interim Report as of June 30, 2017                 Financial Position




Movements in Liabilities
As of June 30, 2017, total liabilities decreased by € 28.2 billion (or 2 %) compared to year-end 2016.

The overall reduction was primarily driven by a € 92.2 billion decrease in negative market values from derivative financial in-
struments primarily due to the same factors as the movements in positive market values from derivative financial instruments as
discussed above.

A € 7.2 billion decrease in long term debt also contributed to the overall decrease. Constrained funding markets has led to raise
liquidity at slower rate than in previous years.

These decreases were partly offset by a € 33.5 billion increase in brokerage and securities related payables, primarily due to
the same factors as the movements in brokerage and securities related receivables as discussed above.

Deposits increased by € 31.3 billion during the period, mainly as a result of campaigns in our Private and Commercial Bank as
well as cash management initiatives in our transaction bank.

Trading liabilities increased by € 11.4 billion, mainly attributable to increased trading activities in EU & US rates business, in line
with seasonal patterns.

Other short term borrowings increased by € 2.9 billion mainly driven by wholesale funding and prime brokerage activities.

Similar to total assets, the impact of foreign exchange rate movements during the period is already embedded in the overall
movements in liabilities as discussed in this section.



Liquidity
Liquidity reserves amounted to € 285 billion as of June 30, 2017 (compared to € 219 billion as of December 31, 2016). We
maintained a positive internal liquidity stress result as of June 30, 2017 (under the combined scenario), and our Liquidity Cover-
age Ratio for June 30, 2017 was 144 % (compared to 128 % as of December 31, 2016).



Equity
Total equity as of June 30, 2017 increased by € 6.4 billion compared to December 31, 2016. The main factor contributing to this
development was a capital increase of € 8.0 billion from the issuance of 687.5 million new common shares in April 2017. Fur-
ther contributing to the increase was net income attributable to Deutsche Bank shareholders and additional equity components
of € 1.0 billion. Partly offsetting were a net loss from exchange rate changes of € 1.6 billion (relating especially to the U.S. dol-
lar), cash dividends paid to Deutsche Bank shareholders of € 392 million as well as coupons paid on additional equity compo-
nents of € 288 million net of tax.



Pro-forma Regulatory Capital
Our regulatory capital and related capital and leverage ratios as of June 30, 2017 are presented on a pro-forma basis to reflect
the € 8 billion gross proceeds of the capital raise completed in April 2017, the inclusion of which the ECB formally approved on
July 26, 2017 (see “Risk Report: Regulatory Capital” for further details).

Our Common Equity Tier 1 (CET 1) capital according to CRR/CRD 4 as of June 30, 2017 increased in the first half of 2017 by €
4.9 billion to € 52.6 billion. Risk-weighted assets (RWA) according to CRR/CRD 4 were € 354.2 billion as of June 30, 2017,
compared with € 356.2 billion at the end of 2016. The increase in CRR/CRD 4 CET 1 capital resulted in a CRR/CRD 4 CET 1
capital ratio of 14.9 % as of June 30, 2017, compared with 13.4 % at the end of 2016.

Our fully loaded CRR/CRD 4 CET 1 capital as of June 30, 2017 amounted to € 50.1 billion, € 7.8 billion higher compared to €
42.3 billion as of December 31, 2016. RWA according to CRR/CRD 4 fully loaded were € 355.1 billion as of June 30, 2017
compared with € 357.5 billion at the end of 2016. Due to the increase in CET 1 capital our fully loaded CRR/CRD 4 CET 1
capital ratio as of June 30, 2017 increased to 14.1 % as compared to 11.8 % at year-end 2016.

Further details on the development of Regulatory Capital and RWA can be found in the section “Risk Report” of this report
under chapter “Regulatory Capital”.

Deutsche Bank                                     Strategy
Interim Report as of June 30, 2017                Management and Supervisory Board




Strategy
Our core business model is that of a global bank combined with a strong home base in Germany. We serve the real economy
needs of our corporate, institutional, asset management and private clients, providing services in transaction banking, corporate
finance and capital markets, asset management, wealth management and retail banking. This business model remains the
foundation of our strength and long-term growth prospects.

To further strengthen the bank and reposition for growth, we announced a number of additional strategic measures. Decisions
agreed by the Management Board and Supervisory Board on March 5, 2017 aim to reinforce the bank’s roots in its home mar-
ket of Germany and its position as a leading European bank with global reach:

   having capital levels the sufficiency of which are beyond question,
   having a leading CIB franchise with the scale and strength to successfully compete and grow globally,
   occupying the number one private and commercial banking position in our home market of Germany,
   giving our world class Deutsche AM division operational segregation that can support accelerated growth,
   reducing the size of our corporate center and cost base in part through more front to back alignment and shifting large por-
   tions of infrastructure functions to the business divisions, and
   shifting our earnings and business mix more towards stable businesses.

Geographically, Germany will remain our anchor – our home market where we intend not only to maintain, but to further expand
our leading position in all three of our business divisions. Fundamentally, we intend to retain our global capabilities, but plan to
focus those capabilities where our management believes our franchise is the strongest, the growth potential the largest, and the
potential risk adjusted returns the highest.

   PCB will be primarily focused in Germany, with wealth management businesses around the world.
   Given the global nature of our core corporate clients, we intend to retain CIB capabilities across Germany and EMEA (ex-
   Germany), the U. S. and Canada, and in Asia Pacific (APAC). While we intend to have a global institutional client footprint,
   we expect to be primarily focused on Germany and EMEA (ex-Germany) where our competitive franchise is strongest. We
   also intend to maintain a strong, but more focused U. S. footprint.
   Deutsche AM will continue to provide a full suite of investment management services in Germany and the wider EMEA
   region, while enhancing its specialist capabilities in the U.S. and APAC.

As part of our updated strategy in March 2017 we have adopted new financial targets. The new targets are:

   Adjusted costs of € 22 billion by 2018, and € 21 billion by 2021, which includes the adjusted costs of Postbank
   Post-tax Return on Average Tangible Equity of approximately 10 % in a normalized operating environment
   CRR/CRD 4 Common Equity Tier 1 capital ratio (fully loaded) of comfortably above 13 %
   CRR/CRD 4 leverage ratio according to transitional rules (phase-in) of 4.5 %
   Targeting a competitive dividend payout ratio for the financial year 2018 and thereafter

Deutsche Bank                                    1 – Management Report
Interim Report as of June 30, 2017




Progress against strategic goals and targets
On March 5, 2017, we announced our intention to increase our equity capital to remove any doubts about our soundness, and
create a basis for growth. We successfully completed our capital increase with gross proceeds of € 8 billion in April 2017.

We completed the reorganisation of our business operations into three distinct units, with the goals of strengthening each,
enhancing client coverage, improving market share and driving efficiencies and growth:

The new corporate client-led Corporate & Investment Bank (CIB): Our Sales & Trading, Advisory, Financing and Transaction
Banking businesses have been merged into one integrated division. The new corporate division “Corporate & Investment Bank”
combines the former segments “Global Markets” and “Corporate & Investment Banking”. This forms the basis for the intended
shift of our focus in this business to corporate clients while retaining a strong, but more focused institutional client base.

Largest Private & Commercial Bank (PCB) in Germany: The corporate division “Private & Commercial Bank” combines the
businesses with private and commercial clients of Deutsche Bank and Postbank, which formerly had been reported separately,
and the wealth management activities for wealthy clients, foundations and family offices. The foundations for Postbank integra-
tion have been laid with the establishment of a joint integration and governance structure. The strategic intention to streamline
operations and structures and to share one IT platform has also been confirmed. Meanwhile, execution of existing strategic
initiatives continues, including implementation of a new go-to-market model, head office streamlining and branch closures in
Germany (177 branches in PCC Germany closed as of the end of June 2017).

An operationally segregated Deutsche Asset Management (Deutsche AM): The corporate division Deutsche Asset Manage-
ment remains materially unchanged and contains the asset management activities of Deutsche Bank. It focuses on providing
investment solutions to individual investors and institutions that serve them. Steps to float up to 25 % of Deutsche AM are being
taken with the gradual execution of the defined target legal entity structure and operating model.

Significant year on year reduction in costs highlights progress against announced cost targets. In the first half of 2017 adjusted
costs were 6 % lower compared to the same time period in 2016. This is in part driven by tangible reductions in full-time equiva-
lents (FTE) and increased cost discipline. Overall, internal FTE have dropped from 99,744 in December 2016 to 96,652 in June
2017.

Deutsche Bank                                      Outlook
Interim Report as of June 30, 2017                 The Global Economy




Outlook
The following section should be read in conjunction with the Outlook section in the Management Report provided in the Annual
Report 2016.



The Global Economy
Global investors faced mixed signals from macro data and market signals in recent months. These were especially accentuated
by an apparent dichotomy between lower inflation due to falling oil prices, lower yields and flatter curves on the one hand, and
continuing solid global growth and firm risk sentiment on the other. We believe the declining core inflation trend is due primarily
to temporary factors, particularly in the U.S., and the uptrend should resume given the solid economic growth momentum. The
outlook on global growth has changed only slightly since the beginning of the year. We believe that U.S. growth has accelerated
less than expected, driven by lower odds of Trump’s policy agenda being implemented, but we still expect deregulation and
modest fiscal stimulus to support an above-trend growth. The weaker outlook for the U.S. is compensated by an improved
economic growth outlook for the eurozone and China. Our expectations regarding the market-friendly results of the recent
elections in Europe were confirmed. Moreover, the intervention to resolve ailing banks in Italy has lowered the risk. However, in
the UK the outcome of the exit negotiations from the European Union (Brexit) has become more binary: the risk of a soft Brexit
has risen, but so has that of a hard Brexit.

We expect global economic growth to see a moderate increase to 3.6 % in 2017, after having achieved a growth rate of only
3.1 % in 2016, its weakest increase since the global financial crisis in 2009. We anticipate that the global inflation rate will likely
accelerate to 5.3 % in 2017, primarily due to the upswing in commodity prices. For industrialized countries, we expect growth to
accelerate to 2.1 %, and consumer prices to increase by 1.8 % in 2017. We expect economic growth in the emerging markets
to increase to 4.7 % in 2017, with inflation at 7.8 %.

The economic outlook for the eurozone has improved, and we anticipate GDP growth to move to 1.9 % in 2017. The key elec-
tions in major EU Member States and the related uncertainty did not dampen economic activity in the first half of 2017. As the
political risks have not materialized so far, the economy has already gained some momentum. For instance, the German econ-
omy unexpectedly saw a positive development, and we expect a GDP growth of 1.6 %, on the back of a strong labour market.
While, the supportive monetary policy stance of the European Central Bank (ECB) should remain in place for some time, we
expect that the ECB will take further normalization steps. After reducing its monthly asset purchases to € 60 billion from April
2017 onwards, in their forward guidance in June 2017, the ECB removed the language from the policy statement indicating
interest rates could go lower in the future. We expect a further normalization in 2018 if consumer prices continue to rise, at least
at a slow pace.

We expect economic growth in the U.S. to accelerate to 2.4 % in 2017. We anticipate that the labour market will perform fairly
well, with a continued increase in employment and a drop in the unemployment rate to close to full employment. Wage growth
may strengthen further with positive impulses for private consumption. Investments may rise due to an ongoing dynamic hous-
ing market and also higher equipment investment as the dampening effect of low oil prices on the energy sector and the strong
U.S.-Dollar fade. Potentially, the economy may also benefit from the combination of tax cuts, deregulation and infrastructure
investments being considered by the executive branch. We expect consumer prices to rise by 2.3 %. The Federal Reserve's
monetary policy should continue to provide stimulus for the U.S. economy despite its gradual policy tightening. We expect that
the Fed will hike its policy rate three times to 1.375 % by year-end 2017.

The Japanese economy is expected to expand at a similar pace to the prior year at 1.2 %, as private capital investment benefits
from the improving global trade backdrop. We anticipate that this should continue to be buoyed by the country's monetary policy.
The Bank of Japan is focused on controlling the yield curve, and the pace of monetary expansion is expected to slow down. We
expect inflation to be at 0.4 %. Economic growth in the emerging markets is projected to rise to 4.7 % in 2017; in Asia (exclud-
ing Japan), we expect it to remain more or less unchanged at 6.1 %, with inflation at 2.4 %. We expect the Chinese economy to
further decelerate to 6.7 % in 2017 on the back of lower property sales dampening the fiscal space and deleveraging in the
credit market. Inflation should remain low at 1.7 %.




                                                                                                                                    21
Deutsche Bank                                     1 – Management Report
Interim Report as of June 30, 2017




The Banking Industry
Interest rates in Europe are expected to remain low for longer than had been anticipated several months ago. Despite the ongo-
ing steps to normalize monetary policy in the U.S., it is therefore expected that the banks' net interest income situation will not
fundamentally improve in the near future. At the same time, however, the low interest rates and robust economic growth should
boost lending to households and businesses and keep loan losses at a low level. The challenging earnings outlook notwith-
standing, further restructuring efforts by the banks are likely to improve the profit situation in comparison with the weak prior-
year period. The largest uncertainties facing the sector are the exit negotiations between the EU and the UK on the one hand,
and the pace and extent of the ECB's likely change in monetary policy on the other. Abruptly raising interest rates could place a
considerable strain on the banks.

The healthy economic environment means that German banks are expected to continue benefiting from an expanding lending
business in the next twelve months. The growth in both corporate and retail lending should continue to outpace the overall GDP
growth figure for the eurozone. However, the rapid expansion in deposits observed to date may wane.

In the U.S., questions surrounding the direction of economic and fiscal policy have grown in recent months. The weaker outlook
for growth in the overall economy goes hand in hand with less favorable prospects for lending. Nevertheless, a certain cooling-
down in corporate lending, which had continued to see extraordinarily strong growth in 2016, is not expected to prove to be
problematic.

In Japan, the next twelve months are not expected to see any major change of pace in the traditional banking business. In
China, the very high rate of expansion in lending to the private sector could see a moderate deceleration in line with a slight
economic slowdown. At the same time, this also presents the most acute risk of a severe correction.

We expect the regulatory outlook to focus on completing existing work streams at the EU and global level. Finalising these may
be impacted by the potential for greater fragmentation resulting from UK’s exit from the EU, and a revised regulatory approach
from the new U.S. administration. The international regulatory agenda, including the finalisation of the Basel III package and risk
weightings for sovereign exposures, could see wavering momentum and a less clear commitment to local implementation of
international rules, including rules that have already been proposed in the EU such as the Fundamental Review of the Trading
Book (FRTB), Net Stable Funding Ratio (NSFR) and Standardised Approach to Counterparty Credit Risk (SACCR). EU initia-
tives such as revisions to the leverage ratio minimum level for systemic banks, forthcoming transition in loan loss accounting
from the "incurred loss" method to the "expected loss" method, and the establishment of the level of own funds and liabilities
eligible for bail-in that must be held by major banks in the eurozone for a potential resolution (Total Loss Absorbing Capital /
Minimum Requirement for Own Funds and Eligible Liabilities - TLAC/MREL) should progress as planned. Risks associated with
UK’s exit is expected to sharpen focus on the EU capital markets union, calibrating the Bank Recovery and Resolution Directive
(BRRD) and reviewing the role of the European Supervisory Authorities (ESAs). These could provide stability to mitigate some
risks associated with the UK’s exit from the EU and broader potential disruptions.

(RWA) are expected to increase in the financial year 2017, notably from operational risk, methodology changes and selected
business growth.

Revenues of our operating businesses are expected to be lower than last year. This reflects our expectation that market volatili-
ty and related client activity remain muted, whereas our macro outlook remains broadly positive. This comparison excludes the
contributions of the significant businesses exits Abbey Life, PCS and HuaXia disposed of in 2016, as well as credit-spread
driven Debt Valuation Adjustments and valuation of our own debt.

We are committed to work towards our target of 10 % Post-tax Return on Average Tangible Equity, assuming a normalized
revenue environment and on the basis of the achievement of our cost targets. The measures currently underway, and planned
for implementation in 2017 and the following years, are key elements for reaching that target. However, given the continued
burden, mainly from litigation and restructuring costs, we currently expect only a moderate improvement of our Post-tax Return
on Average Tangible Equity in 2017.

As part of the Group-wide cost reduction program, we plan to complete our branch network optimization, deliver efficiencies
through digitalization of processes and streamline the infrastructure functions to reduce headcount and cost. In parallel, we plan
to continue our investments in strengthening the control functions and the supporting infrastructure environment. We are target-
ing approximately € 22 billion in adjusted costs in 2018, which includes Postbank’s adjusted costs. We target a further reduction
to approximately € 21 billion by 2021. In 2017, we expect to see net cost reductions flow through from investments made last
year, as well as from the impact of expected headcount reductions, and the successful completion of our NCOU disposals. We
plan to return to our normal compensation programs in 2017 after the Management Board decided for 2016 to substantially limit
bonus payments. Overall, we expect our adjusted costs to further decline in 2017 compared to 2016.

We target a competitive dividend payout ratio for the financial year 2018 and thereafter. If we report sufficient levels of distribut-
able profits under our stand-alone financial statements in accordance with German accounting rules (HGB) for the fiscal year
2017, we expect to recommend at least a dividend payment of € 0.11 per share for the fiscal year 2017.

By the nature of our business, we are involved in litigation, arbitration and regulatory proceedings and investigations in Germa-
ny and in a number of jurisdictions outside Germany, especially in the U.S. Such matters are subject to many uncertainties.
While we have resolved a number of important legal matters and made progress on others, we expect the litigation and en-
forcement environment to continue to be challenging. Although in the first half of 2017 we recorded virtually no litigation ex-
pense we anticipate these expenses to be higher in the second half of 2017.

Our Business Segments
In accordance with our strategy announcement on March 5, 2017, we reorganized our business operations under a new divi-
sional structure comprising the divisions Corporate & Investment Bank (CIB), Private & Commercial Bank (PCB), and Deutsche
Asset Management (Deutsche AM) in the second quarter of 2017. The following paragraphs contain the outlook of our business
segments in that structure.



Corporate & Investment Bank (CIB)
Our Global Markets and existing Corporate & Investment Banking divisions have been combined into a new Corporate & In-
vestment Bank division.

For full year 2017, we expect Corporate & Investment Bank revenues to be slightly lower compared to full year 2016. We ex-
pect Sales & Trading (FIC) revenues to be slightly lower. A supportive macroeconomic backdrop and stable credit fundamentals
are expected to drive higher revenues from Credit products, whilst diverging monetary policies are expected to drive demand
for Rates products. However, the low volatility environment experienced in the second quarter of 2017 may continue into the
second half of the year and result in persistently lower client activity levels. We expect Sales & Trading (Equity) revenues to be
slightly lower year on year. Sluggish Cash Equity volumes may persist into the second half of the year. In addition low volatility
may potentially impact client demand for Derivatives products. We expect Financing revenues to be slightly lower year-on-year.

We expect Origination & Advisory revenues to improve slightly with growth expected to come from our equity origination busi-
nesses, building on positive momentum seen at the beginning of the year, with debt origination and advisory staying broadly flat.
In Global Transaction Banking, higher funding charge allocations, a persistently low interest rate environment in Europe and the
strategic rationalization of our perimeter are expected to continue to weigh on revenues. For full year 2017, we expect these to
be lower compared to the prior year.

We remain committed to reduce costs across CIB and drive platform efficiency while enhancing regulatory compliance, control
and conduct. Our adjusted cost base (excluding litigation, impairment of goodwill and intangibles, severance and restructuring)
is trending slightly lower for the full year 2017, in part due to favourable FX movements. The new CIB division expects to
achieve a reduction of its adjusted costs by 2018 through streamlining its infrastructure to achieve further efficiencies in the CIB
front and middle office functions and the supporting infrastructure, without exiting whole business lines. Nonetheless, in the near
term, we expect to continue to face pressure on our returns as we continue to experience RWA increases, mainly driven by
Operational Risk RWA and methodology changes. We will maintain our focus on regulatory compliance, know-your-client (KYC)
and client on-boarding process enhancement, system stability and control and conduct.

Risks to outlook include the implementation of MiFID2 in 2018 and the potential impact on our business model of the exit pro-
cess of the UK from the European Union. Uncertainty around central bank policies and ongoing regulatory developments also
pose a risk, while challenges such as event risks and a slow-down in client activity may also impact financial markets. Despite
this, we believe that continued execution on the announced strategic priorities will position us favourably to face potential chal-
lenges and capitalize on future opportunities.



Private & Commercial Bank (PCB)
The corporate division Private & Commercial Bank combines our business areas Private & Commercial Clients Germany, Pri-
vate & Commercial Clients International, Postbank and Wealth Management.

Our objective in this reorganized division with nearly 25 million clients is to provide a seamless client coverage while continuing
our investments into digitalization to broaden the client base and to drive efficiencies. In Germany, we are targeting to be the
leading German private and commercial bank by integrating the Private & Commercial Clients Germany and Postbank busi-
nesses. The improvement of our technology platform in Italy is one of the current objectives in our Private & Commercial Clients
International business. In Wealth Management, we will continue to follow our strategy which includes to invest in key growth
markets, e.g. Asia, Americas and the Middle East as well as selected de-risking and transformation initiatives.




24
Deutsche Bank                                     Outlook
Interim Report as of June 30, 2017                Our Business Segments




In our PCC businesses, we expect a similar year-on-year decline as in 2016 in net interest income driven by lower deposit
products, which continue to suffer from the low interest rate environment throughout 2017. PCC’s commission and fee income
is anticipated to be higher in 2017 after it had been negatively impacted by a turbulent market environment with low client activi-
ty in 2016. Excluding specific items like the sale of VISA Europe in 2016, we expect total revenues in the PCC businesses to
remain essentially flat despite the negative impact from the continued low interest rate environment. In our Postbank business,
we also expect that net interest income will continue to be negatively impacted by the low interest rate environment. The impact
of lower interest rates on our deposit revenues will be reduced by growth in loan revenues in line with our strategy to selectively
expand our loan book. We expect commission and fee income to be higher in 2017 in light of our strengthened advisory ap-
proach and the revised pricing model for accounts. Postbank’s remaining income is expected to be on a significantly lower level
compared to 2016 mainly due to reduced asset disposal activities and the negative impact of the termination of a legacy Trust
Preferred Security in 2017. Excluding these specific items, we expect Postbank’s total revenues to remain essentially flat com-
pared to 2016 levels despite the negative impact of the low interest rate environment. Wealth Management business is ex-
pected to have higher revenues in 2017. The impact of the lower revenue base after the sale of the Private Client Services
(PCS) unit in 2016 is expected to be more than compensated by gains from workout activities in the Sal. Oppenheim franchise.
Excluding these items, we expect revenues to be slightly higher supported by growth in our key regions in Germany and Asia
Pacific. Excluding specific items mentioned above, we expect PCB’s total net revenues to remain essentially flat despite the
negative impact from the continued low interest rate environment.

In 2017 we expect PCB’s RWA to be slightly higher.

Our loan loss provisions were low in 2016 following sales of selected portfolios. In 2017, we expect loan loss provisions to be
slightly lower, based on similar effects combined with a specific release in Postbank in the second quarter 2017.

In line with our strategy and our objectives of standardization and simplification, we plan to continue to optimize our branch
networks and to improve our efficiency, and thus expect the number of employees to further decline in 2017. The resulting
decrease in compensation expenses combined with the deconsolidation impact from the sale of the PCS business in 2016 is
expected to reduce our cost base. Inflation rate effects as well as continued investment spending (e.g. in digitalization) will
partly counteract this. Our noninterest expenses will also continue to be influenced by further transformation and integration
measures, including measures which will be taken for the foreseen integration of our German retail operations.

Uncertainties around our performance in 2017 include slower economic growth in our main operating countries and higher than
expected volatility in equity and credit markets, which could adversely affect investor risk appetite and asset flow as well as
decline in interest rates globally. In addition, fierce competition, tighter regulatory requirements as well as delays in the execu-
tion of our strategic projects could negatively impact both our revenue generating capacity and our cost base.



Deutsche Asset Management (Deutsche AM)
In Deutsche Asset Management, our outlook centers around the potential market impacts of initial Brexit negotiations, policy
developments in the U.S., and China’s response to mounting corporate debt; as well as markets being influenced by ongoing
geopolitical events such as monetary policy continuing to diverge and oil production changes. Bouts of further volatility across
markets are possible. Throughout this uncertain period for investors, Deutsche AM remains focused on delivering as a trusted
partner and solutions provider to our clients.

As announced in March 2017, we are taking steps to prepare Deutsche AM for a partial initial public offering within 24 months in
order to unlock the intrinsic value of our business. We continue to view longer term industry growth trends favoring our capabili-
ties in beta (passive) products, alternative investments and active multi-asset solutions, areas where we believe we can grow
market share both in our home market and abroad. With the net new asset growth reported in the first six months of 2017 and
clarity around not only the future structure of Deutsche AM, but also the improved capital outlook for Deutsche Bank Group, we
see client confidence increasing and are cautiously optimistic about asset development for the remainder of 2017. In the medi-
um term, we expect industry assets to grow, albeit at a lower organic rate than in prior years, and profit pools to be challenged
by fee compression, rising costs of regulation, and competitive dynamics. In the face of this challenge, we intend to balance
growth through product and coverage expansion with initiatives to ensure an efficient cost base and operating platform.

Excluding prior year items not expected to recur, such as the proceeds from the sale of Asset Management India, the write up
relating to HETA exposure and Abbey Life revenues, we expect full year 2017 underlying revenues to be slightly higher year-
on-year driven by revenue growth from Active and Alternatives businesses reflecting improved market conditions. Following the
sale of Abbey Life in the fourth quarter of 2016, noninterest expenses are expected to be significantly lower as policyholders,
benefits and claims ceased following the sale, and we do not anticipate a repeat of material impairments incurred in 2016. Risks
to Deutsche AM’s outlook include the pace of net new assets growth, the exposure to global macroeconomic growth and the
political developments including Brexit negotiations, and policy developments in the U.S.

Risks and Opportunities
We have reflected in our Outlook risks and opportunities that we believe are likely to occur. The following section focuses on
future trends or events that may result in downside risk or upside potential from what we have anticipated in our Outlook.

Our aspirations are subject to various external and internal factors. In particular, timely and complete achievement of our strate-
gic aspirations may be adversely impacted by the reduced revenue-generating capacities of some of our core businesses in the
current challenging macro-economic and market environment, the ongoing headwinds posed by regulatory reforms and/or the
effects on us of our legal and regulatory proceedings.

Depending on whether the economic and market conditions worsen or improve compared to forecasted levels, this could either
adversely affect or positively impact our business, results of operations or strategic plans.

Continued elevated levels of political uncertainty could have unpredictable consequences for the financial system and the
greater economy and could contribute to an unwinding of European integration, potentially leading to declines in business levels,
write-downs of assets and losses across our businesses. Our ability to protect ourselves against these risks is limited.

The overall macro-economic impact of the United Kingdom’s decision to leave the European Union, which will depend on Eu-
rope’s political response to Brexit, is difficult to predict. In general, we expect a prolonged period of uncertainty regarding the
UK’s future status with the EU. Therefore, weaker investment and thereby slower economic growth are expected to persist
during the UK exit negotiations. As a consequence, we will closely monitor the developments and their impact on our business
and operating model. This may potentially require taking impairments on assets.

The direct costs and related business impacts described in this section and in our Outlook, should they be significantly greater
than we currently expect, would impact the “available distributable items” (ADI) calculation for Deutsche Bank AG, which forms
the basis for payment capacity on our Additional Tier 1 (AT1) securities. If Deutsche Bank AG’s solo HGB results do not provide
sufficient ADI, this would impact our ability to make distributions on our AT1 instruments. This could lead to higher funding costs
for us and adversely affect perceptions of us in the markets, with potential adverse effects on our results of operations and
financial condition. Such impacts may also put increasing pressure on our capital, liquidity and other regulatory ratios. Also, if
we do not report sufficient levels of distributable profits under our stand-alone financial statements in accordance with German
accounting rules (HGB), this would impact our ability to pay dividends.

A downgrade in our credit rating could affect our funding costs and business activities, although we are unable to predict
whether this would be the case or the extent of any such effect.

The regulatory reforms enacted and proposed in response to weaknesses in the financial sector together with the increased
regulatory scrutiny and discretion will impose material costs on us, create significant uncertainty for us and may adversely affect
our business plans as well as our ability to execute our strategic plans. In addition, regulatory reforms in respect of resolvability
or resolution measures may also impact our shareholders and creditors.

Regulators can also impose capital surcharges, for example, as result of the annual Supervisory Review and Evaluation Pro-
cess (SREP), to reflect the additional risks posed by deficiencies in our control environment. In extreme cases, they can even
suspend our permission to operate within their jurisdictions. Furthermore, implementing enhanced controls may result in higher
regulatory compliance costs that could offset or exceed efficiency gains.

Tax administrations have been focusing on the eligibility of taxpayers for reduced withholding taxes on dividends in connection
with certain cross-border lending or derivative transactions, with the German Federal Ministry of Finance recently having issued
administrative guidance in this area. In addition, we are subject to a number of legal proceedings, tax examinations and regula-
tory investigations, whose outcome is difficult to estimate and which may substantially and adversely affect our planned results
of operations, financial condition and reputation. If these matters are resolved on terms that are more adverse to us than we
expect, in terms of their costs or necessary changes to our businesses, or if related negative perceptions concerning our busi-
ness and prospects and related business impacts increase, we may not be able to achieve our strategic objectives or we may
be required to change them.

Although we have devoted significant resources to develop our risk management policies, procedures and methods, including
with respect to market, credit, liquidity and operational risk, they may not be fully effective in mitigating our risk exposures in all
economic market environments or against all types of risks, including risk that we fail to identify or anticipate.
